| [Ch 271](chapters/271.md) | **Chlamydia pneumoniae** | `—` | **Regimen** | If adherence to a multiday regimen is not a concern, azithromycin administered over a 5-day course (500 mg on day 1 followed by 250 mg daily for 4 days) is an alternative and may limit the develop ment of resistance. |

| [Ch 271](chapters/271.md) | **Chlamydia pneumoniae** | `—` | **Regimen** | If the initial empiric regimen did not include macro lides, retreatment with an azithromycin-based regimen may be indicated. |

| [Ch 275](chapters/275.md) | **Scrub Typhus (Orientia tsutsugamushi)** | `—` | **Regimen** | TREATMENT AND SUPPORTIVE CARE The recommended treatment regimen for scrub typhus is doxycycline (4 mg/kg/day PO or IV divided every 12 hours; maximum: 200 mg/ day). |
| [Ch 275](chapters/275.md) | **Scrub Typhus (Orientia tsutsugamushi)** | `—` | **Mg/kg** | Alternative regimens include tetracycline (25-50 mg/kg/day PO divided every 6 hours; maximum: 2 g/day) or chloramphenicol (50-100 mg/kg/day divided every 6 hours IV; maximum: 4 g/24 hr). |

| [Ch 276](chapters/276.md) | **Typhus Group Rickettsioses (276.1 Murine (Endemic or Flea-Borne) Typhus (Rickettsia typhi) Mega** | `—` | **Immunoglobulin** | Thus paired (acute and convalescent) serology to demonstrate a fourfold rise in immunoglobulin (Ig) G antibody titer by IFA remains the standard for confirming acute infection. |

| [Ch 276](chapters/276.md) | **Typhus Group Rickettsioses (276.1 Murine (Endemic or Flea-Borne) Typhus (Rickettsia typhi) Mega** | `—` | **Doxycycline** | However, in adult patients, a prospective randomized clinical trial showed that either a 3or 7-day course of doxycycline was superior to 3 days of azithromycin for fever clearance. |
| [Ch 276](chapters/276.md) | **Typhus Group Rickettsioses (276.1 Murine (Endemic or Flea-Borne) Typhus (Rickettsia typhi) Mega** | `—` | **Doxycycline** | Therefore the time-honored recommended treatment for murine typhus remains doxycycline (4 mg/kg/day divided every 12 hours PO or IV; maximum: 200 mg/day). |
| [Ch 277](chapters/277.md) | **Ehrlichiosis and Anaplasmosis** | `—` | **Treatment of choice** | The treatment of choice is doxycycline (4 mg/kg/day divided every 12 hours PO or IV; maxi mum: 200 mg/day). |

| [Ch 278](chapters/278.md) | **Q Fever (Coxiella burnetii)** | `—` | **Immunoglobulin** | The diagnosis of primary (acute) Q fever is most easily and commonly confirmed by testing acute and convalescent sera (3-6 weeks apart), which show a fourfold increase in indirect fluorescent immunoglobulin G antibody titers to phase II C. burnetii antigens. |

| [Ch 282](chapters/282.md) | **Malassezia** | `—` | **Fluconazole** | Various dosing regimens have been used with success, including fluconazole 300 mg weekly for 2-4 weeks, flucon azole as a single 400-mg dose, and itraconazole 200 mg daily for 5-7 days or 100 mg daily for 2 weeks. |

| [Ch 284](chapters/284.md) | **Histoplasmosis (Histoplasma capsulatum)** | `—` | **Voriconazole** | Voriconazole is the preferred first-line therapy. |

| [Ch 286](chapters/286.md) | **Coccidioidomycosis (Coccidioides Species)** | `—` | **Amphotericin** | Newborns with blastomycosis should be treated with amphotericin B deoxycholate 1 mg/kg/day. |

| [Ch 286](chapters/286.md) | **Coccidioidomycosis (Coccidioides Species)** | `—` | **Amphotericin** | Children with severe disease, immunodeficiency, or immunosuppression should be treated with amphotericin B deoxycholate 0.7-1.0 mg/kg/day or lipid amphotericin B 3-5 mg/kg/day until there is clinical improve ment, generally 7-14 days, and then itraconazole 10 mg/kg/day (maxi mum: |

| [Ch 286](chapters/286.md) | **Coccidioidomycosis (Coccidioides Species)** | `—` | **Fluconazole** | Itraconazole can increase the risk for spontaneous abortion, and fluconazole can cause cranio facial defects resembling Antley-Bixler syndrome. |
| [Ch 287](chapters/287.md) | **Paracoccidioides brasiliensis** | `—` | **Fluconazole** | After completion of therapy, patients should be evaluated yearly for at least 2 years because some patients relapse (28% of adults who completed 12 months of fluconazole, 18% for itraconazole). |

| [Ch 288](chapters/288.md) | **Sporotrichosis (Sporothrix schenckii)** | `—` | **Fluconazole** | Fluconazole has also been used, but high doses (≥600 mg/day) and longer treatment periods are required. |
| [Ch 288](chapters/288.md) | **Sporotrichosis (Sporothrix schenckii)** | `—` | **Voriconazole** | A small number of patients have been treated with other azoles, including voriconazole, posaconazole, and isavuconazole. |

| [Ch 289](chapters/289.md) | **Mucormycosis** | `—` | **Treatment of choice** | TREATMENT Although comparative trials and extensive experience in children are not available, itraconazole is the recommended treatment of choice for infections outside the central nervous system. |
| [Ch 289](chapters/289.md) | **Mucormycosis** | `—` | **Mg/kg** | The recommended dosage for children is 5-10 mg/kg/day orally, with an initial maximum dose of 200 mg daily, which may be increased up to 400 mg daily if there is no initial response. |
| [Ch 289](chapters/289.md) | **Mucormycosis** | `—` | **Amphotericin** | Amphotericin B is the treatment of choice for pulmonary infections, disseminated infec tions, central nervous system disease, and infections in immuno compromised persons. |

| [Ch 326](chapters/326.md) | **Primary Amebic Meningoencephalitis** | `—` | **Amphotericin** | Until 2013, there had been only two known survivors in North America, with treatment regimens of amphotericin B, either alone or in combination with other agents such as rifampin, chloramphenicol, fluconazole, ketoconazole, and dexamethasone. |
| [Ch 326](chapters/326.md) | **Primary Amebic Meningoencephalitis** | `—` | **Amphotericin** | The recom mended drug treatment for primary amebic meningoencephalitis by the CDC includes intravenous and intrathecal amphotericin B, oral miltefosine, along with azithromycin, fluconazole, rifampin, and dexamethasone. |
| [Ch 326](chapters/326.md) | **Primary Amebic Meningoencephalitis** | `—` | **Management** | Early identification, early initiation of combi nation therapy, and aggressive management of increased intracranial pressure remain key elements for a successful outcome. |
| [Ch 326](chapters/326.md) | **Primary Amebic Meningoencephalitis** | `—` | **Amphotericin** | Strains of Acanthamoeba isolated from fatal cases are usu ally susceptible in vitro to pentamidine, ketoconazole, and flucytosine and less so to amphotericin B. |
| [Ch 326](chapters/326.md) | **Primary Amebic Meningoencephalitis** | `—` | **Fluconazole** | One patient was successfully treated with sulfadiazine and fluconazole, and another was successfully treated with intravenous pentamidine followed by oral itraconazole. |

| [Ch 328](chapters/328.md) | **Giardiasis and Balantidiasis** | `—` | **Metronidazole** | A Cochrane metaanalysis comparing metronidazole and metronidazole plus aspiration in uncomplicated amebic liver abscess showed that there is insufficient evi dence to make any recommendation for or against this approach. |
| [Ch 328](chapters/328.md) | **Giardiasis and Balantidiasis (328.1 Giardia duodenalis Chandy C. John)** | `—` | **Immunoglobulin** | Human milk contains glycoconjugates and secre tory immunoglobulin A antibodies that may provide protection to nursing infants against Giardia. |
| [Ch 329](chapters/329.md) | **Cryptosporidium, Cystoisospora, Cyclospora, and Microsporidia** | `—` | **Trimethoprim** | Cystoisosporiasis responds promptly to treatment with oral trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX: 5 mg TMP and 25 mg SMX/kg/dose [maximum: 160 mg TMP and 800 mg SMX/ dose] bid for 10 days). |
| [Ch 329](chapters/329.md) | **Cryptosporidium, Cystoisospora, Cyclospora, and Microsporidia** | `—` | **Regimen** | Cipro floxacin or a regimen of pyrimethamine alone or with folinic acid is effective in patients intolerant of sulfonamide drugs. |
| [Ch 329](chapters/329.md) | **Cryptosporidium, Cystoisospora, Cyclospora, and Microsporidia** | `—` | **Treatment of choice** | The treatment of choice for cyclosporiasis is TMP-SMX (5 mg TMP and 25 mg SMX/kg/dose bid [maximum 160 mg TMP and 800 mg SMX/dose] for 10 days.) Ciprofloxacin or nitazoxanide is effective in patients intolerant of sulfonamide drugs. |
| [Ch 330](chapters/330.md) | **Trichomoniasis (Trichomonas vaginalis)** | `—` | **Mg/kg** | Albendazole (adult dose 400 mg bid PO for 3 weeks; for children, 7.5 mg/kg body weight [maximum 400 mg/dose] bid PO) is usually effective against E. intestinalis infection but is ineffective against infec tion caused by some microsporidial species. |
| [Ch 331](chapters/331.md) | **Leishmaniasis (Leishmania)** | `—` | **Metronidazole** | TREATMENT In the United States, metronidazole and tinidazole are used; in other countries, secnidazole, azanidazole, and ornidazole are also used. |
| [Ch 331](chapters/331.md) | **Leishmaniasis (Leishmania)** | `—` | **Metronidazole** | Both metronidazole (single-dose regimen of 2 g orally as a single dose for adolescents and adults; alternative regimen, 500 mg orally bid for 7 days) and tinidazole (single 2 g dose orally in adolescents and adults) are used as first-line treatments. |
| [Ch 331](chapters/331.md) | **Leishmaniasis (Leishmania)** | `—` | **Regimen** | For children infected before adoles cence, the recommended regimen is metronidazole 15 mg/kg/day divided in three doses orally for 7 days; tinidazole is not approved for dosing in younger children. |
| [Ch 331](chapters/331.md) | **Leishmaniasis (Leishmania)** | `—` | **Metronidazole** | For HIV-infected patients, the 7-day course of metronidazole is superior to and recommended over the single-dose regimen. |
| [Ch 331](chapters/331.md) | **Leishmaniasis (Leishmania)** | `—` | **Metronidazole** | Recent studies have shown that single-dose metronidazole is less effective than a multidose regimen in women. |
| [Ch 332](chapters/332.md) | **African Trypanosomiasis (Sleeping Sickness; Trypanosoma brucei Complex)** | `—` | **Immunoglobulin** | Examination of the blood during this stage may show anemia, leukopenia with relative monocytosis, and elevated levels of immunoglobulin M (IgM). |
| [Ch 332](chapters/332.md) | **African Trypanosomiasis (Sleeping Sickness; Trypanosoma brucei Complex)** | `—` | **Mg/kg** | The dose for subse quent IV injections is 20 mg/kg (maximum 1 g) administered on days 1, 3, 7, 14, and 21. |
| [Ch 332](chapters/332.md) | **African Trypanosomiasis (Sleeping Sickness; Trypanosoma brucei Complex)** | `—` | **Mg/kg** | Pentamidine isethionate (4 mg/kg/day intramuscularly [IM] daily or on alternate days for 7-10 days) concentrates to high levels in trypanosomes and is highly trypanocidal. |
| [Ch 333](chapters/333.md) | **American Trypanosomiasis (Chagas Disease; Trypanosoma cruzi)** | `—` | **Treatment of choice** | Stage 2 Treatment Combination eflornithine and nifurtimox (NECT) is the current treatment of choice for T. brucei gambiense CNS infection. |
| [Ch 333](chapters/333.md) | **American Trypanosomiasis (Chagas Disease; Trypanosoma cruzi)** | `—` | **Mg/kg** | Eflornithine is given at 400 mg/kg/day IV divided every 12 hours for 7 days, along with nifurtimox, 15 mg/kg/day orally divided every 8 hour for 10 days. |
| [Ch 333](chapters/333.md) | **American Trypanosomiasis (Chagas Disease; Trypanosoma cruzi)** | `—` | **Mg/kg** | If nifurtimox is unavailable, eflornithine monotherapy can be given at 400 mg/kg/day IV divided every 6 hours for 14 days. |
| [Ch 333](chapters/333.md) | **American Trypanosomiasis (Chagas Disease; Trypanosoma cruzi)** | `—` | **Mg/kg** | Treatment of children is initiated at 0.36 mg/kg IV once daily, with gradually escalating doses every 1-5 days to 3.6 mg/kg once daily; treatment is usually 10 doses (18-25 mg/kg total dose). |

| [Ch 339](chapters/339.md) | **Trichuriasis (Trichuris trichiura)** | `—` | **Drug of choice** | TREATMENT Albendazole (400 mg orally for 3 days, for all ages) is the drug of choice and is safe and effective, in part because it is poorly absorbed from the gastrointestinal tract. |

| [Ch 341](chapters/341.md) | **Strongyloidiasis (Strongyloides stercoralis)** | `—` | **Treatment of choice** | Albendazole (400 mg orally with a repeat dose 2 week later for all age-groups) is the treatment of choice and results in cure rates exceeding 90%. |
| [Ch 341](chapters/341.md) | **Strongyloidiasis (Strongyloides stercoralis)** | `—` | **Μg/kg** | Ivermectin (200 μg/kg/day once daily orally for 2 days) is the drug of choice for uncomplicated strongyloidiasis. |

| [Ch 343](chapters/343.md) | **Other Tissue Nematodes** | `—` | **Mg/kg** | Global programs to control and ultimately eradicate lymphatic filariasis from endemic populations outside of sub-Saharan Africa currently recommend triple drug treatment with one or two annual doses of diethylcarbamazine (6 mg/kg orally once), albendazole (400 mg orally once), an |
| [Ch 343](chapters/343.md) | **Other Tissue Nematodes** | `—` | **Μg/kg** | A single dose of ivermectin (150 μg/kg orally) is the drug of choice and clears O. volvulus microfilariae from the skin for several months but has no effect on the adult worm. |
| [Ch 343](chapters/343.md) | **Other Tissue Nematodes** | `—` | **Treatment of choice** | Diethylcarbamazine is the treatment of choice for loiasis, as there is evidence that it kills both microfilariae and adult worms and results in a sustained decrease in microfilarial intensity after treatment. |

| [Ch 344](chapters/344.md) | **Toxocariasis (Visceral and Ocular Larva Migrans)** | `—` | **Metronidazole** | Metronidazole (25 mg/kg/day orally divided into three doses for 10 days; maximum dose: 750 mg) decreases local inflammation. |
| [Ch 344](chapters/344.md) | **Toxocariasis (Visceral and Ocular Larva Migrans)** | `—` | **First-line** | There is no well-documented effective chemotherapy, although albendazole (400 mg orally twice daily for 21 days) as first-line therapy or ivermectin (200 μg/kg for 2 days) as an alternative is recommended without or with surgical removal. |
| [Ch 344](chapters/344.md) | **Toxocariasis (Visceral and Ocular Larva Migrans)** | `—` | **Treatment of choice** | Sur gical resection of the Gnathostoma is the major mode of therapy and the treatment of choice. |

| [Ch 766](chapters/766.md) | **Rat Bite Fever** | `—` | **Pediatric Clinical Management** | CLINICAL COURSE The incubation period for the streptobacillary form of rat bite fever is variable, ranging from 3-10 days. |
